I keep reaching for more food, even while I am sitting there thinking to myself, Patricia, stop, this is enough. But I just can't seem to stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have control over what you're eating and how much you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: No, not really. Like I was just saying, it's like I can't control it at all. Once I start eating, my brain just kind of shuts off. I'll literally be sitting there telling myself, 'Patricia, you need to stop, this is way too much,' but my hands just keep reaching for more food. I just don't have any control over it once it starts.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty or ashamed after one of these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much so. Afterward, I just feel... really embarrassed and disgusted with myself. I try to be a very disciplined person, and when that happens, it feels like I've just blown that whole image. I'll just sit there feeling awful, thinking about how I really should have just gone for a run or... or done yoga instead of doing what I just did.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight due to these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, my weight might fluctuate a pound or two here and there, but I haven't noticed any major changes. I'll just sit there feeling awful, thinking about how I really should have just gone for a run or... or done yoga instead of doing what I just did.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight due to these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, my weight might fluctuate a pound or two here and there, but I haven't noticed any major changes. I do try to stay pretty active with running and things like that during the day, so... yeah, my weight has actually stayed relatively stable despite all of this.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating really quickly during these late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. I eat so fast during those times. Usually, I'm just standing right there at the kitchen island, maybe scrolling through some work emails on my phone, and I barely even taste the food. Sometimes I'll look down and the containers are just... empty, before I've really even registered what I was eating.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel anxious, stressed, or upset right before one of these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I haven't noticed feeling particularly anxious or upset right before it happens. I mean, my job is demanding, so there's always a bit of a baseline stress during the week, but... right before I start eating? No, I don't think so. I'm usually just standing there, maybe finishing up some tasks on my phone, and it just sort of happens. I wouldn't say there's a big emotional trigger right in that exact moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating more rapidly than you would during a normal meal during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, like I mentioned a minute ago, it's definitely a lot faster than how I'd eat a normal meal. With a regular dinner, I sit down and actually take my time. But during these episodes... I'm just standing there at the kitchen island, usually scrolling through work emails, and eating so fast that I barely even taste it. It's just like I said, I'll look down and the containers are already empty before my brain has even registered what I was doing. So, yes, it's much more rapid.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. And some weeks, it actually happens twice.\n\nDoctor: During these late-night eating episodes, do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you've started?\n\nPatient: Yes, that is exactly what happens. Once I start... especially if it's after a tense night with my husband, it feels like the decision-making part of me just goes offline. I keep reaching for more food, even while I am sitting there thinking to myself, Patricia, stop, this is enough. But I just can't seem to stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have control over what you're eating and how much you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: No, not really. Like I was just saying, it's like I can't control it at all. Once I start eating, my brain just kind of shuts off. I'll literally be sitting there telling myself, 'Patricia, you need to stop, this is way too much,' but my hands just keep reaching for more food. I just don't have any control over it once it starts.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty or ashamed after one of these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much so. Afterward, I just feel... really embarrassed and disgusted with myself. I try to be a very disciplined person, and when that happens, it feels like I've just blown that whole image. I'll just sit there feeling awful, thinking about how I really should have just gone for a run or... or done yoga instead of doing what I just did.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight due to these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, my weight might fluctuate a pound or two here and there, but I haven't noticed any major changes. I do try to stay pretty active with running and things like that during the day, so... yeah, my weight has actually stayed relatively stable despite all of this.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating really quickly during these late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. I eat so fast during those times. Usually, I'm just standing right there at the kitchen island, maybe scrolling through some work emails on my phone, and I barely even taste the food. Sometimes I'll look down and the containers are just... empty, before I've really even registered what I was eating.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel anxious, stressed, or upset right before one of these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I haven't noticed feeling particularly anxious or upset right before it happens. I mean, my job is demanding, so there's always a bit of a baseline stress during the week, but... right before I start eating? No, I don't think so. I'm usually just standing there, maybe finishing up some tasks on my phone, and it just sort of happens. I wouldn't say there's a big emotional trigger right in that exact moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating more rapidly than you would during a normal meal during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, like I mentioned a minute ago, it's definitely a lot faster than how I'd eat a normal meal. With a regular dinner, I sit down and actually take my time. But during these episodes... I'm just standing there at the kitchen island, usually scrolling through work emails, and eating so fast that I barely even taste it. It's just like I said, I'll look down and the containers are already empty before my brain has even registered what I was doing. So, yes, it's much more rapid.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating in secret during these late-night episodes because you're ashamed of how much you're eating?\n\nPatient: I don't think so, no. I mean, I live alone, so I'm by myself anyway when it happens. It's not like I'm trying to hide it from anyone in the house, because there isn't anyone else here. I do feel bad about it afterward, but I wouldn't say I'm doing it in secret because of shame. It's just... I'm alone in my kitchen late at night.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes of eating large amounts of food only happen at night, or do they also occur during the day?\n\nPatient: No, it's pretty much just at night. During the day, I'm usually at work, so I'm busy with meetings or just eating a regular lunch at my desk. I haven't really had this happen during the daytime at all. It's... yeah, it's really just those late nights when I'm home.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or ashamed during these episodes because you feel like you've lost control over your eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Not so much during, but right after. Afterward, I feel really embarrassed and just... disgusted with myself. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because... well, I've been having this issue with eating. After everyone goes to bed, I’ll tell myself I’m just having some leftovers. But then I realize I’ve eaten half a tray of catered pasta from work, the dessert from a client dinner, and just whatever snacks were in the pantry. And it's all in one sitting.\n\nDoctor: How often do these late-night eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: Well, for the last three months, it's been... at least one night almost every week. I usually end up eating like this after an argument, or if I've had a late work event. And some weeks, it actually happens twice.\n\nDoctor: During these late-night eating episodes, do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you've started?\n\nPatient: Yes, that is exactly what happens. Once I start... especially if it's after a tense night with my husband, it feels like the decision-making part of me just goes offline. I keep reaching for more food, even while I am sitting there thinking to myself, Patricia, stop, this is enough. But I just can't seem to stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have control over what you're eating and how much you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: No, not really. Like I was just saying, it's like I can't control it at all. Once I start eating, my brain just kind of shuts off. I'll literally be sitting there telling myself, 'Patricia, you need to stop, this is way too much,' but my hands just keep reaching for more food. I just don't have any control over it once it starts.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty or ashamed after one of these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much so. Afterward, I just feel... really embarrassed and disgusted with myself. I try to be a very disciplined person, and when that happens, it feels like I've just blown that whole image. I'll just sit there feeling awful, thinking about how I really should have just gone for a run or... or done yoga instead of doing what I just did.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight due to these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, my weight might fluctuate a pound or two here and there, but I haven't noticed any major changes. I do try to stay pretty active with running and things like that during the day, so... yeah, my weight has actually stayed relatively stable despite all of this.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating really quickly during these late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. I eat so fast during those times. Usually, I'm just standing right there at the kitchen island, maybe scrolling through some work emails on my phone, and I barely even taste the food. Sometimes I'll look down and the containers are just... empty, before I've really even registered what I was eating.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel anxious, stressed, or upset right before one of these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I haven't noticed feeling particularly anxious or upset right before it happens. I mean, my job is demanding, so there's always a bit of a baseline stress during the week, but... right before I start eating? No, I don't think so. I'm usually just standing there, maybe finishing up some tasks on my phone, and it just sort of happens. I wouldn't say there's a big emotional trigger right in that exact moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating more rapidly than you would during a normal meal during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, like I mentioned a minute ago, it's definitely a lot faster than how I'd eat a normal meal. With a regular dinner, I sit down and actually take my time. But during these episodes... I'm just standing there at the kitchen island, usually scrolling through work emails, and eating so fast that I barely even taste it. It's just like I said, I'll look down and the containers are already empty before my brain has even registered what I was doing. So, yes, it's much more rapid.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating in secret during these late-night episodes because you're ashamed of how much you're eating?\n\nPatient: I don't think so, no. I mean, I live alone, so I'm by myself anyway when it happens. It's not like I'm trying to hide it from anyone in the house, because there isn't anyone else here. I do feel bad about it afterward, but I wouldn't say I'm doing it in secret because of shame. It's just... I'm alone in my kitchen late at night.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes of eating large amounts of food only happen at night, or do they also occur during the day?\n\nPatient: No, it's pretty much just at night. During the day, I'm usually at work, so I'm busy with meetings or just eating a regular lunch at my desk. I haven't really had this happen during the daytime at all. It's... yeah, it's really just those late nights when I'm home.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or ashamed during these episodes because you feel like you've lost control over your eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Not so much during, but right after. Afterward, I feel really embarrassed and just... disgusted with myself. I spend all day trying to be this disciplined person, keeping everything together at work, and then I just blow it completely. I'll sit there looking at the empty containers and think about how I should have just gone for a run or done some yoga instead of doing what I just did. So, yeah, there's definitely a lot of guilt and shame once it's over.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost control over what you're eating during these late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It's... it's like once I start, I just can't stop. Especially if it's been a really tense night, like if my husband and I get into an argument or something. It feels like the rational, decision-making part of my brain just completely shuts off. I'll just keep reaching for more food, even while I'm literally thinking in my head, \"Patricia, stop, this is enough.\" But I just keep going anyway.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I'm alone in my kitchen late at night.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes of eating large amounts of food only happen at night, or do they also occur during the day?\n\nPatient: No, it's pretty much just at night. During the day, I'm usually at work, so I'm busy with meetings or just eating a regular lunch at my desk. I haven't really had this happen during the daytime at all. It's... yeah, it's really just those late nights when I'm home.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or ashamed during these episodes because you feel like you've lost control over your eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Not so much during, but right after. Afterward, I feel really embarrassed and just... disgusted with myself. I spend all day trying to be this disciplined person, keeping everything together at work, and then I just blow it completely. I'll sit there looking at the empty containers and think about how I should have just gone for a run or done some yoga instead of doing what I just did. So, yeah, there's definitely a lot of guilt and shame once it's over.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost control over what you're eating during these late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It's... it's like once I start, I just can't stop. Especially if it's been a really tense night, like if my husband and I get into an argument or something. It feels like the rational, decision-making part of my brain just completely shuts off. I'll just keep reaching for more food, even while I'm literally thinking in my head, \"Patricia, stop, this is enough.\" But I just keep going anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your late-night eating episodes are usually triggered by emotional events, such as arguments with your husband?\n\nPatient: I'm actually not sure. I know I just mentioned the arguments, but... I don't think it's always triggered by something emotional like that. Sometimes it happens after a completely normal, quiet evening where nothing went wrong at all. I haven't really tracked it, to be honest, so I can't say for sure if there's a specific emotional trigger every time. Sometimes I'm not upset about anything, and it just sort of... happens anyway.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I keep reaching for more food, even while I am sitting there thinking to myself, Patricia, stop, this is enough. Like I was just saying, it's like I can't control it at all. Once I start eating, my brain just kind of shuts off. I'll literally be sitting there telling myself, 'Patricia, you need to stop, this is way too much,' but my hands just keep reaching for more food. Afterward, I just feel... really embarrassed and disgusted with myself. I try to be a very disciplined person, and when that happens, it feels like I've just blown that whole image. And it's all in one sitting.\n\nDoctor: How often do these late-night eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: Well, for the last three months, it's been... at least one night almost every week. I usually end up eating like this after an argument, or if I've had a late work event. And some weeks, it actually happens twice.\n\nDoctor: During these late-night eating episodes, do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you've started?\n\nPatient: Yes, that is exactly what happens. Once I start... especially if it's after a tense night with my husband, it feels like the decision-making part of me just goes offline. I keep reaching for more food, even while I am sitting there thinking to myself, Patricia, stop, this is enough. But I just can't seem to stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have control over what you're eating and how much you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: No, not really. Like I was just saying, it's like I can't control it at all. Once I start eating, my brain just kind of shuts off. I'll literally be sitting there telling myself, 'Patricia, you need to stop, this is way too much,' but my hands just keep reaching for more food. I just don't have any control over it once it starts.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty or ashamed after one of these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much so. Afterward, I just feel... really embarrassed and disgusted with myself. I try to be a very disciplined person, and when that happens, it feels like I've just blown that whole image. I'll just sit there feeling awful, thinking about how I really should have just gone for a run or... or done yoga instead of doing what I just did.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight due to these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, my weight might fluctuate a pound or two here and there, but I haven't noticed any major changes. I do try to stay pretty active with running and things like that during the day, so... yeah, my weight has actually stayed relatively stable despite all of this.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating really quickly during these late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. I eat so fast during those times. Usually, I'm just standing right there at the kitchen island, maybe scrolling through some work emails on my phone, and I barely even taste the food. Sometimes I'll look down and the containers are just... empty, before I've really even registered what I was eating.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel anxious, stressed, or upset right before one of these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I haven't noticed feeling particularly anxious or upset right before it happens. I mean, my job is demanding, so there's always a bit of a baseline stress during the week, but... right before I start eating? No, I don't think so. I'm usually just standing there, maybe finishing up some tasks on my phone, and it just sort of happens. I wouldn't say there's a big emotional trigger right in that exact moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating more rapidly than you would during a normal meal during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, like I mentioned a minute ago, it's definitely a lot faster than how I'd eat a normal meal. With a regular dinner, I sit down and actually take my time. But during these episodes... I'm just standing there at the kitchen island, usually scrolling through work emails, and eating so fast that I barely even taste it. It's just like I said, I'll look down and the containers are already empty before my brain has even registered what I was doing. So, yes, it's much more rapid.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating in secret during these late-night episodes because you're ashamed of how much you're eating?\n\nPatient: I don't think so, no. I mean, I live alone, so I'm by myself anyway when it happens. It's not like I'm trying to hide it from anyone in the house, because there isn't anyone else here. I do feel bad about it afterward, but I wouldn't say I'm doing it in secret because of shame. It's just... I'm alone in my kitchen late at night.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes of eating large amounts of food only happen at night, or do they also occur during the day?\n\nPatient: No, it's pretty much just at night. It's... yeah, it's really just those late nights when I'm home.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or ashamed during these episodes because you feel like you've lost control over your eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Not so much during, but right after. Afterward, I feel really embarrassed and just... disgusted with myself. I'll sit there looking at the empty containers and think about how I should have just gone for a run or done some yoga instead of doing what I just did. So, yeah, there's definitely a lot of guilt and shame once it's over.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost control over what you're eating during these late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It's... it's like once I start, I just can't stop. Especially if it's been a really tense night, like if my husband and I get into an argument or something. It feels like the rational, decision-making part of my brain just completely shuts off. I'll just keep reaching for more food, even while I'm literally thinking in my head, \"Patricia, stop, this is enough.\" But I just keep going anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your late-night eating episodes are usually triggered by emotional events, such as arguments with your husband?\n\nPatient: I'm actually not sure. And it's all in one sitting.\n\nDoctor: How often do these late-night eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: Well, for the last three months, it's been... at least one night almost every week. I usually end up eating like this after an argument, or if I've had a late work event. Once I start... especially if it's after a tense night with my husband, it feels like the decision-making part of me just goes offline. I keep reaching for more food, even while I am sitting there thinking to myself, Patricia, stop, this is enough. Like I was just saying, it's like I can't control it at all. Once I start eating, my brain just kind of shuts off. I'll literally be sitting there telling myself, 'Patricia, you need to stop, this is way too much,' but my hands just keep reaching for more food. I just don't have any control over it once it starts.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty or ashamed after one of these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much so. Afterward, I just feel... really embarrassed and disgusted with myself. I try to be a very disciplined person, and when that happens, it feels like I've just blown that whole image. I'll just sit there feeling awful, thinking about how I really should have just gone for a run or... or done yoga instead of doing what I just did.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight due to these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, my weight might fluctuate a pound or two here and there, but I haven't noticed any major changes. I do try to stay pretty active with running and things like that during the day, so... yeah, my weight has actually stayed relatively stable despite all of this.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating really quickly during these late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. I eat so fast during those times. Usually, I'm just standing right there at the kitchen island, maybe scrolling through some work emails on my phone, and I barely even taste the food. I wouldn't say there's a big emotional trigger right in that exact moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating more rapidly than you would during a normal meal during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, like I mentioned a minute ago, it's definitely a lot faster than how I'd eat a normal meal. I usually end up eating like this after an argument, or if I've had a late work event. Once I start... especially if it's after a tense night with my husband, it feels like the decision-making part of me just goes offline. I keep reaching for more food, even while I am sitting there thinking to myself, Patricia, stop, this is enough. But I just can't seem to stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have control over what you're eating and how much you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: No, not really. Like I was just saying, it's like I can't control it at all. Once I start eating, my brain just kind of shuts off. I'll literally be sitting there telling myself, 'Patricia, you need to stop, this is way too much,' but my hands just keep reaching for more food. I just don't have any control over it once it starts.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty or ashamed after one of these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much so. Afterward, I just feel... really embarrassed and disgusted with myself. I try to be a very disciplined person, and when that happens, it feels like I've just blown that whole image. I'll just sit there feeling awful, thinking about how I really should have just gone for a run or... or done yoga instead of doing what I just did.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight due to these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, my weight might fluctuate a pound or two here and there, but I haven't noticed any major changes. I wouldn't say there's a big emotional trigger right in that exact moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating more rapidly than you would during a normal meal during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, like I mentioned a minute ago, it's definitely a lot faster than how I'd eat a normal meal. I'm just standing there at the kitchen island, usually scrolling through work emails, and eating so fast that I barely even taste it. It's just like I said, I'll look down and the containers are already empty before my brain has even registered what I was doing. And it's all in one sitting.\n\nDoctor: How often do these late-night eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: Well, for the last three months, it's been... at least one night almost every week. I usually end up eating like this after an argument, or if I've had a late work event. And some weeks, it actually happens twice.\n\nDoctor: During these late-night eating episodes, do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you've started?\n\nPatient: Yes, that is exactly what happens. Once I start... especially if it's after a tense night with my husband, it feels like the decision-making part of me just goes offline. I keep reaching for more food, even while I am sitting there thinking to myself, Patricia, stop, this is enough. But I just can't seem to stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have control over what you're eating and how much you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: No, not really. Like I was just saying, it's like I can't control it at all. Once I start eating, my brain just kind of shuts off. I'll literally be sitting there telling myself, 'Patricia, you need to stop, this is way too much,' but my hands just keep reaching for more food. I just don't have any control over it once it starts.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty or ashamed after one of these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much so. Afterward, I just feel... really embarrassed and disgusted with myself. I try to be a very disciplined person, and when that happens, it feels like I've just blown that whole image. I'll just sit there feeling awful, thinking about how I really should have just gone for a run or... or done yoga instead of doing what I just did.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight due to these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, my weight might fluctuate a pound or two here and there, but I haven't noticed any major changes. I do try to stay pretty active with running and things like that during the day, so... yeah, my weight has actually stayed relatively stable despite all of this.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating really quickly during these late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. I eat so fast during those times. Usually, I'm just standing right there at the kitchen island, maybe scrolling through some work emails on my phone, and I barely even taste the food. Sometimes I'll look down and the containers are just... empty, before I've really even registered what I was eating.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel anxious, stressed, or upset right before one of these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I haven't noticed feeling particularly anxious or upset right before it happens. I mean, my job is demanding, so there's always a bit of a baseline stress during the week, but... right before I start eating? No, I don't think so. I'm usually just standing there, maybe finishing up some tasks on my phone, and it just sort of happens. I wouldn't say there's a big emotional trigger right in that exact moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating more rapidly than you would during a normal meal during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, like I mentioned a minute ago, it's definitely a lot faster than how I'd eat a normal meal. With a regular dinner, I sit down and actually take my time. But during these episodes... I'm just standing there at the kitchen island, usually scrolling through work emails, and eating so fast that I barely even taste it. It's just like I said, I'll look down and the containers are already empty before my brain has even registered what I was doing. So, yes, it's much more rapid.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating in secret during these late-night episodes because you're ashamed of how much you're eating?\n\nPatient: I don't think so, no. I mean, I live alone, so I'm by myself anyway when it happens. It's not like I'm trying to hide it from anyone in the house, because there isn't anyone else here. I do feel bad about it afterward, but I wouldn't say I'm doing it in secret because of shame. It's just... It's... it's like once I start, I just can't stop. Especially if it's been a really tense night, like if my husband and I get into an argument or something. I'll just keep reaching for more food, even while I'm literally thinking in my head, \"Patricia, stop, this is enough.\" But I just keep going anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your late-night eating episodes are usually triggered by emotional events, such as arguments with your husband?\n\nPatient: I'm actually not sure.
